Backward stochastic differential equations with nonlinear Young drivers II
Jian Song, Huilin Zhang, Kuan Zhang

Abstract
. This paper continues our previous work (Part I, [ 48 ] ) on the well-posedness of backward stochastic differential equations (BSDEs) involving a nonlinear Young integral of the form ∫ t T g ⁡ ( Y r ) ​ η ​ ( 𝑑 r , X r ) \int_{t}^{T}g(Y_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r}) , with particular focus on the case where the driver η ⁡ ( t , x ) \eta(t,x) is unbounded. To address this setting, we develop a new localization method that extends solvability from BSDEs with bounded drivers to those with unbounded ones. As a direct application, we derive a nonlinear Feynman-Kac formula for a class of partial differential equations driven by Young signals (Young PDEs). Moreover, employing the proposed localization method, we obtain error estimates that compare Cauchy-Dirichlet problems on bounded domains with their whole-space Cauchy counterparts, with special attention to non-Lipschitz PDEs.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
60L20, 60L50, 60H10

1.
Introduction

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space
supporting a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
. Given functions
f
,
g
,
\eta
, and a diffusion process
X
, we consider the following backward stochastic differential equation (BSDE) with the solution pair
(Y_{t},Z_{t})
:

(1.1)

Y_{t}=\xi+\int_{t}^{T}f(r,X_{r},Y_{r},Z_{r})dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{t}^{T}g_{i}(Y_{r})\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t}^{T}Z_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T],

where
\int g(Y_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})
is a
nonlinear Young integral
(we refer to
[
48
]
and the references therein for more details on nonlinear Young integrals). In Part I
[
48
]
, we established the well-posedness of Equation (
1.1
), under the assumption that the driver
\eta(t,x)
is uniformly bounded, that is,
\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\eta(t,x)|<\infty
. In the present work, we aim to go beyond this restriction by allowing
\eta
to grow polynomially in the spatial variable. This broader framework in particular encompasses the important case where
\eta
is realized from a fractional Brownian sheet, thereby capturing a wider class of stochastic signals of interest.

1.1.
Motivations and difficulties

Our motivations stem from the following two problems.

(a) The first motivation is to establish the Feynman-Kac formula for partial differential equations driven by rough signals (rough PDEs) of the following form:

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} -du(t,x)&=\mathcal{L}u(t,x)dt+g(u,\nabla u)\mathbf{M}(dt,x),\quad(t,x)\in(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
u(T,x)&=h(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{L}
is a second-order differential operator and
\mathbf{M}=(\mathrm{M},\mathbb{M})
is a rough path with spatial parameter (see Nualart-Xia
[
44
]
for the definition).

When
\mathrm{M}
is independent of
x
and
\mathrm{M}(t)
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for some
\alpha\in(1/2,1)
,
Equation (
1.2
) is referred to as a Young PDE since the integral
\int\cdots d\mathbf{M}_{t}
reduces to a Young integral. Gubinelli et al.
[
26
]
first proved that Young evolution equations of the form (
1.2
) admit unique mild solutions. Subsequently, Addona et al.
[
1
]
investigated the regularity of such solutions. The nonlinear Feynman–Kac representation for Young PDEs was derived by Diehl–Zhang
[
20
]
in the case where
\mathrm{M}
is independent of
x
, while Hu–Lê
[
31
]
proved a (linear) Feynman–Kac formula when
\mathrm{M}
depends on
x
.

When
\mathbf{M}
is independent of the spatial parameter, the theory of rough PDEs has primarily been developed in two frameworks. The first treats (
1.2
) as an evolution equation solved in the mild sense (cf.
[
27
,
17
,
16
,
28
,
30
]
). Recently, Liang–Tang
[
40
]
studied the well-posedness of rough stochastic evolution equations driven by mixed signals. The second approach defines the solution
u
as the limit of a sequence
\{u^{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
, where each
u^{n}
solves (
1.2
) with
\mathbf{M}
replaced by
\mathbf{M}^{n}
. Here
\mathbf{M}^{n}
is the canonical lift of a smooth path (see, e.g.,
[
24
, Definition 7.2]
) such that
\mathbf{M}^{n}\to\mathbf{M}
in rough path space. This method allows for more flexible assumptions on
\mathcal{L}
, often relying on tools such as rough flow transformations and the Feynman–Kac formula (see, e.g.,
[
9
,
10
,
23
,
19
]
). More recently, Bugini et al.
[
7
]
solved (
1.2
) in the strong sense via the Feynman–Kac formula, proving that
u\in C^{\alpha\text{-H\"{o}l}}([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}))
for
\alpha<1/2
under mild regularity conditions. Apart from these two approaches, Buckdahn et al.
[
5
]
developed a viscosity framework for fully nonlinear rough PDEs via a transformation along the characteristic equations.

There are comparatively few results on Equation (
1.2
) with a general nonlinear rough driver
\mathbf{M}(t,x)
. Nualart–Xia
[
44
]
investigated linear transport-type equations driven by nonlinear rough integrals, employing inverse transformations derived from nonlinear rough flows. A related contribution is due to Coghi–Nilssen
[
13
]
, who showed that the law of the solution to a McKean–Vlasov SDE with a nonlinear rough driver
uniquely solves a (linear) Fokker–Planck equation with an unbounded rough driver. For the general case where both
g
and
\mathbf{M}
are nonlinear, Equation (
1.2
) has been rarely studied in the literature.

In this paper, as a continuation of
[
48
]
, we investigate the well-posedness of Equation (
1.2
) with
\int\cdots\mathbf{M}(dt,x)
interpreted as a
nonlinear Young integral
, with particular focus on the case where
g(u,\nabla u)=g(u)
is nonlinear and
\mathrm{M}
is unbounded. A natural strategy is to derive a nonlinear Feynman–Kac formula via the associated BSDE, which motivates our study of Equation (
1.1
). We further note that our forthcoming work will be devoted to BSDEs with nonlinear
rough
integrals, which is related to the
rough
PDE (
1.2
).

(b) The second motivation is to establish error estimates for localized PDEs, which concern the convergence rates of solutions under varying boundary conditions (see
[
2
,
35
,
42
,
14
,
37
,
49
,
29
]
). Such estimates are crucial for assessing the accuracy of numerical solutions of parabolic PDEs, as finite-difference methods typically require bounded domains. Consequently, they have numerous applications in mathematical finance, particularly in the numerical computation of option prices (cf. Hilber et al.
[
29
, Section 4.3]
and Lamberton and Lapeyre
[
37
, Section 5.3.2]
). We further note that in the context of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs), especially those driven by noise which is white in time, localization errors have been investigated in works such as
[
25
,
8
]
.

To the best of our knowledge, no such estimates have been established for non-Lipschitz PDEs. More precisely, assume that the Lipschitz constant of the mapping
(y,z)\mapsto f(t,x,y,z)
exhibits polynomial growth in
x
. For a bounded domain
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, consider the truncated Cauchy-Dirichlet problem:

(1.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\partial_{t}u_{D}(t,x)&=\mathcal{L}u_{D}(t,x)+f(t,x,u_{D},\nabla u_{D}),\ (t,x)\in(0,T)\times D,\\
u_{D}(t,x)&=h(x),\text{ for }(t,x)\in\{T\}\times\bar{D}\cup(0,T)\times\partial D.\end{aligned}\right.

Our objective is to prove that

u_{D}
converges to a solution
u
of the associated Cauchy problem as the domain
D
expands to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and to derive an estimate for the convergence rate. This, in turn, naturally leads us to the study of BSDEs with unbounded drivers. The non-Lipschitz nature of Equation (
1.3
) originates from the growth of the coefficient function
f(t,x,y,z)
in the spatial variable
x
, which directly yields an unbounded driver (see Remark
3.3
). Furthermore, by the nonlinear Feynman–Kac representation for PDEs with Dirichlet boundary conditions, the BSDE corresponding to Equation (
1.3
) is terminated at the first exit time of the diffusion process
X
from

D
. Hence, estimating localization errors for non-Lipschitz PDEs reduces to analyzing the convergence of the associated localized BSDEs with unbounded drivers.

The distinction between our BSDE (
1.1
) and related results in the literature—such as rough BSDEs studied by Diehl–Friz
[
18
]
and Young BSDEs considered in Diehl–Zhang
[
20
]
—was already discussed in Part I
[
48
]
. In addition, compared with the bounded case studied in
[
48
]
, the unboundedness of
\eta
gives rise to two major challenges: the loss of Lipschitz continuity of the coefficient function and the potential unboundedness of the solution. We elaborate on these two issues below.

First, since the mapping
y\mapsto g(y)\eta(dr,X_{r})
is no longer Lipschitz, the fixed-point (Picard iteration) method used in
[
48
]
is not applicable. Moreover, existing methods for non-Lipschitz BSDEs (see, e.g.,
[
3
,
4
,
39
]
) cannot be applied to our setting either, as
\eta
possesses only Hölder continuity in time. In fact, when applying the classical linearization method to establish an
a priori
estimate for the solution (as in
[
3
, Lemma 5]
,
[
4
, Lemma 7]
, and
[
39
, Proposition 2.2]
), a crucial step is to show the integrability of
\exp\{\int_{0}^{t}a_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\}
, where
a_{r}:=(g(Y_{r})-g(0))/Y_{r}
. However, the upper bound of the nonlinear Young integral
\int_{0}^{t}a_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})
depends on the
p
-variation of
a_{r}
, and thus on the
p
-variation of
Y_{r}
, which remains unknown in this
a priori
estimate.

Second, the
a priori
estimates developed in
[
20
,
48
]
require the boundedness of the solution

Y
. However, this assumption becomes invalid when
\eta
is unbounded, as demonstrated by the explicit solution
Y_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\eta_{i}(ds,X_{s})\right]
obtained in the special case
\xi=0
,
f(\cdot)\equiv 0
and
g(\cdot)\equiv 1
, which generally exhibits unboundedness. We stress that this work removes the boundedness requirement on the terminal condition
\xi
, thereby addressing an open problem posed in
[
20
, Remark 3]
regarding the extension of BSDE theory to unbounded terminal values.

To overcome the aforementioned difficulties, in this paper we employ a new localization method to solve BSDEs. More precisely, we study the convergence of a sequence of localized BSDEs truncated at time
T_{D}
, with
T_{D}
being the first exit time of the diffusion process
X
from
D
. For
t\leq T_{D}
, since the integrator
\eta(dt,X_{t})
depends only on the mapping
x\mapsto\eta(\cdot,x)
for
x\in D
, the solvability of each localized BSDE reduces to the case of BSDEs with bounded drivers, which was studied in Part I
[
48
]
. The terminal value of a localized BSDE, denoted by
\xi^{D}
, is then chosen so that
\xi^{D}\to\xi
as
D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

This localization analysis relies primarily on the regularity properties of BSDE solutions. Indeed, for
D\subset D^{\prime}
and
t\leq T_{D}
, estimating the difference
Y^{D}_{t}-Y^{D^{\prime}}_{t}
reduces to controlling its terminal value

|\xi^{D}-Y^{D^{\prime}}_{T_{D}}|\leq|\xi^{D}-\xi^{D^{\prime}}|+|\xi^{D^{\prime}}-Y^{D^{\prime}}_{T_{D}}|
. The convergence of
|\xi^{D}-\xi^{D^{\prime}}|
is by assumption.
Moreover, regularity results show that the
k
-th moment of
\xi^{D^{\prime}}-Y^{D^{\prime}}_{T_{D}}
can be bounded in terms of moments of
T_{D}-T_{D^{\prime}}
, which decays at the rate
\exp\{-C\text{dist}(\partial D,0)^{2}\}
, assuming
\langle X\rangle_{T}
is bounded. By establishing quantitative estimates for BSDE solutions, particularly for the
p
-variation norm of
Y^{D}
, this exponential decay ensures that
|Y^{D}-Y^{D^{\prime}}|
becomes arbitrarily small as
D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, thereby proving the convergence of
Y^{D}
. We remark that this localization method, which appears to be new even in the deterministic BSDE case, also plays a key role in the study of error estimation for localized PDEs (see Section
3.2
).

We would like to point out that this localization method still relies on the assumption that
g(\cdot)
is bounded (see Remark
2.5
).
Indeed, the unboundedness of the diffusion process
X
would otherwise compromise the integrability of the integral
\int g(Y_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})
. Nevertheless, in the typical case
g(y)=y
, the linear BSDE can be solved directly by constructing its explicit solution.

1.2.
A summary of applications

We conclude this introduction by summarizing two applications.

(i)
In Section
3.1
, we derive the nonlinear Feynman-Kac formula by establishing the connection between the solution to the nonlinear Young BSDE (
1.1
) and the solution to the Young PDE (
1.2
), where
\mathbf{M}(t,x)
is replaced by
\eta(t,x)
.
The solution to Equation (
1.2
) is defined as the unique limit of solutions to the PDEs with Dirichlet boundary conditions and smooth drivers. As a specific example of Equation (
1.2
), we take
\eta
to be a realization of a fractional Brownian sheet
B
with Hurst parameters
H_{0}
in time and
(H,\dots,H)
in space. In this case, Equation (
1.2
) can be interpreted as an SPDE driven by
\partial_{t}B(t,x)
in the Stratonovich sense. Consequently, we establish the well-posedness of the equation and derive the nonlinear Feynman-Kac formula for SPDEs under the conditions

H_{0}+H/2>1
and
dH<2H_{0}+1
.

(ii)
In Section
3.2
, by using the localization method developed in the study of BSDEs, we prove that the unique viscosity solution of PDE (
1.3
) (with
D
replaced by
D_{n}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d};|x|<n\}
) converges to a solution
u
of the PDE on the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular, the coefficients of the limiting PDE can be non-Lipschitz. Let
u^{n}
denote the solution to the PDE on the domain
D_{n}
. Then, we have the following error estimates for the localized PDEs

|u^{n}(t,x)-u(t,x)|\leq C\exp\left\{-n^{2}/C+C|x|^{2}\right\},\ \text{ for some constant }\ C>0.

This paper is organized as follows. For the case when the driver
\eta(t,x)
is unbounded, the existence and the uniqueness of the solution to nonlinear BSDEs are studied in Section
2.1
and Section
2.2
, respectively. The linear BSDE is studied in Section
2.3
. Finally, applications are presented in Section
3
, and some miscellaneous results are summarized in Appendix
A
,
B
, and
C
.

1.3.
Notations

In this subsection, we gather the notations that will be used in this article.

•

\lesssim
:
The notation
f\lesssim g
means
f\leq Cg
for some generic constant
C>0
, and
f\lesssim_{\zeta}g
represents
f\leq C_{\zeta}g
for some constant
C_{\zeta}>0
depending on
\zeta
.

•

Probability space:

Let
T
be a fixed positive real number. Let
\{W_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be a
d
-dimensional standard
Brownian motion on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, and let
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be the augmented filtration generated by
W
. We use the notation
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\cdot]:=\mathbb{E}\left[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]
.

•

L^{k}
-norms:
For a random vector
\xi
, we write
\|\xi\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}
for the
L^{k}
-norm of
\xi
with
k\geq 1
, and the essential supremum norm is denoted by

\|\xi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}:=\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega}|\xi(\omega)|.

Here
|\cdot|
denotes the Euclidean norm for both vectors and matrices.

•

Spaces of functions:
For functions
g:[s,t]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the uniform norms are denoted by

\|g\|_{\infty;[s,t]}:=\esssup_{r\in[s,t]}|g_{r}|\text{ and }\|f\|_{\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}}:=\esssup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(y)|.

For
k\geq 1
, denote by
L^{k}([s,t];\mathbb{R}^{m})
the space of functions on
[s,t]
equipped with the norm

\|g\|_{L^{k}(s,t)}:=\Big\{\int_{s}^{t}|g_{r}|^{k}dr\Big\}^{\frac{1}{k}}.

We write

g\in C([s,t];\mathbb{R}^{m})
if
g
is continuous;
f\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
if
f
is
k
-th continuously differentiable. We denote by
f\in C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
if
f\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
with bounded partial derivatives up to the
k
-th order;
f\in C^{\text{Lip}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
if it is Lipschitz continuous. We denote

C^{p\text{-var}}([s,t];\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\left\{g\in C([s,t];\mathbb{R}^{m})\text{ such that }\|g\|_{p\text{-var};[s,t]}<\infty\right\},

where the
p
-variation is denoted by

\|g\|_{p\text{-var};[s,t]}:=\Big(\sup_{\pi}\sum_{[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\in\pi}|g_{t_{i+1}}-g_{t_{i}}|^{p}\Big)^{1/p}.

Here, the supremum is taken over all partitions
\pi:=\{[t_{i},t_{i+1}];s=t_{1}<t_{2}<...<t_{n}=t\}
.

For
\gamma\in(0,1]
, We denote

C^{\gamma\text{-H\"{o}l}}([s,t];\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\left\{g\in C([s,t];\mathbb{R}^{m})\text{ such that }\|g\|_{\gamma\text{-H\"{o}l};[s,t]}<\infty\right\},

where the
\gamma
-Hölder norm is defined as

\|g\|_{\gamma\text{-H\"{o}l};[s,t]}:=\sup_{a,b\in[s,t];a\neq b}\frac{|g_{a}-g_{b}|}{|a-b|^{\gamma}}\,.

When
m=1
, we may omit the range space
\mathbb{R}^{m}=\mathbb{R}
for the above-mentioned spaces of functions. For instance, we write
C^{\text{Lip}}([s,t])
for
C^{\text{Lip}}([s,t];\mathbb{R})
.

•

Spaces of the driver
\eta
:
We introduce some seminorms for the driver
\eta:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
(see also Hu-Lê
[
31
]
). Given parameters
\tau,\lambda\in(0,1],\ \beta\in[0,\infty)
, denote

\displaystyle\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda;\beta}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s<t\leq T\\
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n};x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|\eta(s,x)-\eta(t,x)-\eta(s,y)+\eta(t,y)|}{|t-s|^{\tau}|x-y|^{\lambda}(1+|x|^{\beta}+|y|^{\beta})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s<t\leq T\\
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{|\eta(s,x)-\eta(t,x)|}{|t-s|^{\tau}(1+|x|^{\beta+\lambda})}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq t\leq T\\
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n};x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|\eta(t,y)-\eta(t,x)|}{|x-y|^{\lambda}(1+|x|^{\beta}+|y|^{\beta})},

\displaystyle\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s<t\leq T\\
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n};x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|\eta(s,x)-\eta(t,x)-\eta(s,y)+\eta(t,y)|}{|t-s|^{\tau}|x-y|^{\lambda}}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s<t\leq T\\
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{|\eta(s,x)-\eta(t,x)|}{|t-s|^{\tau}}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq t\leq T\\
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n};x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|\eta(t,y)-\eta(t,x)|}{|x-y|^{\lambda}}.

Here,
\tau
and
\lambda
are (weighted) Hölder exponents in time and in space, respectively. We introduce the following spaces of functions

\begin{split}&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ C^{\tau,\lambda}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\left\{\eta:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ such that }\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda}<\infty\right\},\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ C^{\tau,\lambda;\beta}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\left\{\eta:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ such that }\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda;\beta}<\infty\right\}.\end{split}

Clearly,
C^{\tau,\lambda}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\subseteq C^{\tau,\lambda;\beta}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m}).

Remark 1.1
.

In particular, if we assume
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\eta(0,x)|<\infty
, then
\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda}<\infty
implies that
|\eta(t,x)|
is uniformly bounded for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see
[
48
, Lemma A.2]
). Letting
\tilde{\eta}(t,x):=\eta(t,x)-\eta(0,x)
, we have
\tilde{\eta}(0,x)\equiv 0
and
\tilde{\eta}(dt,x_{t})=\eta(dt,x_{t})
. Thus, we may assume throughout the paper that
\eta(0,x)\equiv 0
for Equation (
1.1
), and then the condition
\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda}<\infty
yields the uniform boundedness of
|\eta(t,x)|
. We also remark that
\|\cdot\|_{\tau,\lambda;0}
differs from
\|\cdot\|_{\tau,\lambda}
, with
\|\cdot\|_{\tau,\lambda;0}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\tau,\lambda}
.

Remark 1.2
.

A typical example of
\eta
one may keep in mind is a realization of a fractional Brownian sheet
\{B(t,x),(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
with Hurst parameters
H_{i}\in(0,1),i=0,1,\dots,n
, which is a centered Gaussian random field with covariance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[B(t,x)B(s,y)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\left(|t|^{2H_{0}}+|s|^{2H_{0}}-|t-s|^{2H_{0}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(|x_{i}|^{2H_{i}}+|y_{i}|^{2H_{i}}-|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{2H_{i}}\right).

More details can be found in, e.g.,

[
48
, Lemma A.4]
.

•

Seminorms for right continuous with left limits (RCLL) processes:

Let
[s,t]\subset[0,T]
be a fixed interval, and
T_{1},T_{0}
be two stopping times that satisfy
s\leq T_{1}\leq T_{0}\leq t
. Assume that
X:\Omega\times[s,t]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an RCLL adapted process. For
p\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, denote

m^{(s,t)}_{p,k}(X;[T_{1},T_{0}]):=\left\{\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega,u\in[s,t]}\mathbb{E}_{u}\left[\|X\|^{k}_{p\text{-var};[T_{1}\vee(u\land T_{0}),T_{0}]}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{k}}.

In the following, we will omit the superscripts
s
and
t
, if there is no ambiguity.

•

Spaces of
(Y,Z)
:
Now, we introduce the norm that will be used for the solution
(Y,Z)
of Eq. (
1.1
). Let
[s,t]\subseteq[0,T]
be a fixed interval. Denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}([s,t]):=\big\{(y,z)\text{ such that }

\displaystyle y:\Omega\times[s,t]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ is continuous and adapted, and }

\displaystyle z:\Omega\times[s,t]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}\text{ is progressively measurable}\big\},

where the dimensions
n
and
m
may vary depending on the context. For
k\geq 1
and
(y,z)\in\mathfrak{B}([s,t])
, we define the norm

\|(y,z)\|_{p,k;[s,t]}:=m_{p,k}(y;[s,t])+\|z\|_{k\text{-BMO};[s,t]}+\|y_{t}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)},

where

\|z\|_{k\text{-BMO};[s,t]}:=\left\{\esssup_{\omega\in\Omega,u\in[s,t]}\mathbb{E}_{u}\left[\left(\int_{u}^{t}\left|z_{r}\right|^{2}dr\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{k}}.

In Part I
[
48
]
, the metric space for the solution of (
1.1
) with a bounded driver is

\mathfrak{B}_{p,k}(s,t):=\Big\{(y,z)\in\mathfrak{B}([s,t]):\ \|(y,z)\|_{p,k;[s,t]}<\infty\Big\},

equipped with the norm
\|(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{p,k;[s,t]}
. When the driver is unbounded, we use the following metric space for the solution of (
1.1
) in this paper:

\mathfrak{H}_{p,k}(s,t):=\left\{(y,z)\in\mathfrak{B}([s,t]):\ \|(y,z)\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,k};[s,t]}<\infty\right\},

where

(1.4)

\|(y,z)\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,k};[s,t]}:=\|y_{t}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}+\left\|\|y\|_{p\text{-var};[s,t]}\right\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}+\Big\|\big|\int_{s}^{t}|z^{2}_{r}|dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}.

Remark 1.3
.

The norm
\|(\cdot,\cdot)\|_{p,k;[s,t]}
is rather stronger than the usual
L^{2}
-norm in the sense that
\|(y,z)\|_{p,k;[s,t]}<\infty
implies the boundedness of
y
, i.e.,
\esssup\limits_{\omega\in\Omega,r\in[s,t]}|y_{r}|<\infty
. Indeed,

(1.5)

\esssup\limits_{\omega\in\Omega,r\in[s,t]}|y_{r}\mathbf{1}_{[T_{1},T_{0}]}(r)|\leq\|y_{T_{0}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+m_{p,1}(y;[T_{1},T_{0}])\leq\|(y,z)\|_{p,1;[T_{1},T_{0}]}.

•

Malliavin derivative:
For a Malliavin differentiable random variable
\xi\in\mathcal{F}_{T}
, we denote by
D_{\cdot}(\xi)
its Malliavin derivative (see, e.g., Nualart
[
43
]
). Denote

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{1,2}:=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\xi\in\mathcal{F}_{T}:\xi\text{ is Malliavin differentiable},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\qquad\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{D}^{1,2}}:=\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\|D_{\cdot}(\xi)\|_{L^{2}([0,T])}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\infty\bigg\}.

2.
BSDEs with unbounded drivers

In this section, we investigate BSDE (
1.1
) with an unbounded driver
\eta\in C^{\tau,\lambda;\beta}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{M})
. The existence and uniqueness of the solution is established in Section
2.1
and Section
2.2
, respectively. The linear case is treated separately in Section
2.3
, as it requires weaker assumptions and exhibits more refined properties due to its linear structure.

We assume
\eta(0,x)\equiv 0
without loss of generality (see Remark (
1.1
)). We make the following assumption to guarantee the existence of the nonlinear Young integral
\int\eta(dr,x_{r})
(see
[
48
, Section 2]
), where
x_{\cdot}\in C^{p\text{-var}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
p>2
.

Assumption (H0)
.

Let
p,\tau,\lambda
be parameters satisfying

p>2,\ \tau\in(1/2,1],\ \lambda\in(0,1],\text{ and }\tau+\frac{\lambda}{p}>1.

In many situations, one can choose
p>2
for the path
x_{t}
to be as close to
2
as we want (e.g., when
x_{t}
is a realization of a semi-martingale), and thus
(H0)
can be replaced by the following weaker assumption:

Assumption (H0’)
.

Let
\tau,\lambda
be parameters satisfying

\tau\in(1/2,1],\ \lambda\in(0,1],\text{ and }\tau+\frac{\lambda}{2}>1.

Now we impose conditions on
X,\xi,\eta
, and the coefficient functions below.

Assumption (A0)
.

Let
x=(x^{1},...,x^{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For BSDE (
1.1
), we assume
X
is given by

(2.1)

X_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(r,X_{r})dW_{r}+\int_{0}^{t}b(r,X_{r})dr,\ t\in[0,T],

where
\sigma(t,x):[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
b(t,x):[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are measurable functions that are globally Lipschitz in
x
and satisfy the following condition:

\sup_{t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{|\sigma(t,x)|\vee|b(t,x)|\right\}\leq L,

for some positive constant
L
.

Assumption (A1)
.

Let
\tau,\lambda,p
be the parameters satisfying Assumption
(H0)
.

(1)

There exists
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that

\beta\geq 0,\ \tau+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{p}>1,\text{ and }\lambda+\beta<\frac{2\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}\tau.

(2)

\eta\in C^{\tau,\lambda;\beta}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{M})
and
g\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{N};\mathbb{R}^{M})
with

\|g\|_{\infty;\mathbb{R}^{N}}\vee\|\nabla g\|_{\infty;\mathbb{R}^{N}}\vee\|\nabla^{2}g\|_{\infty;\mathbb{R}^{N}}\leq C_{1}.

(3)

f(t,x,y,z):[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a function satisfying

(2.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &|f(t,x,0,0)|\leq C_{1}(1+|x|^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}),\\
&|f(t,x_{1},y,z)-f(t,x_{2},y,z)|\leq C_{1}(1+|x_{1}|^{\mu^{\prime}}+|x_{2}|^{\mu^{\prime}})|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\mu},\\
&|f(t,x,y_{1},z_{1})-f(t,x,y_{2},z_{2})|\leq C_{\mathrm{Lip}}\left(|y_{1}-y_{2}|+|z_{1}-z_{2}|\right),\ \end{aligned}\right.

for all
(t,x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
,
i=1,2
, where
C_{1}
,
C_{\mathrm{Lip}}
,
\mu
, and
\mu^{\prime}
are positive constants with
\mu\in(0,1]
.

Remark 2.1
.

If we assume
(H0’)
and condition (1) in
(A1)
with
p>2
, we have

(2.3)

\lambda+\beta<2\tau-1\text{ and }\tau>\frac{3}{4}.

Proof of (
2.3
).

First, it is clear that
\tau+\frac{\lambda}{2}>1
and
\tau+\frac{(1-\varepsilon)}{2}>1
. Next, since
\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon\tau}
is decreasing in
\varepsilon
and, noting that
\varepsilon<2\tau-1
by
\tau+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}>1
, we have
\frac{2(\lambda+\beta)}{2\tau-1}<\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon\tau}<2
,
which yields that
\lambda+\beta<2\tau-1
. Also, noting that
2-2\tau<\lambda\leq\lambda+\beta
, we have

2-2\tau<2\tau-1
and hence
\tau>\frac{3}{4}
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

In Remark
2.1
, we have shown that given
(H0’)
, condition (1) in
(A1)
implies (
2.3
). In this remark, we prove the converse, that is, given
(H0’)
, (
2.3
) yields condition (1) in
(A1)
with
p>2
to be determined. This indicates that, if
p>2
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
2
, then assuming
(H0’)
, (
2.3
) is equivalent to condition (1) in
(A1)
with
p=2
.

Now, assuming that
\tau,\lambda\in(0,1]
and
\beta\geq 0
satisfy
\tau+\frac{\lambda}{2}>1
and
\lambda+\beta<2\tau-1
, we aim to find
p>2
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that
(\tau,\lambda,\beta,\varepsilon,p)
satisfies condition (1) in Assumption
(A1)
. Let
\delta\in(0,2\tau-1)
be a constant. Thus, we can choose
p
sufficiently close to
2
such that
\tau+\frac{1-\varepsilon}{p}>1
with
\varepsilon=2\tau-1-\delta
. Moreover, the last condition of condition
(1)
in
(A1)
holds if we choose
\delta
sufficiently small, since
\lambda+\beta<2\tau-1
and

\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon\tau}=\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{(2\tau-\delta)(\lambda+\beta)}{(2\tau-1-\delta)\tau}<\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{(2\tau-\delta)(2\tau-1)}{(2\tau-1-\delta)\tau}=2.

Remark 2.3
.

In the proof of Remark
2.1
, we have
\lambda+\beta<2\tau-1
, and hence
\frac{\lambda+\beta+1}{\tau}<2.

This fact, together with the condition
\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon\tau}<2
in Assumption
(A1)
, implies that

\frac{\lambda+\beta+(\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1)}{\tau}<2,

which will be used in
2.1.
The existence
of Theorem
1
. As a consequence, the term
\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1
also appears in Assumption
(A1)
for
f(t,x,0,0)
and in the increment conditions for
|\Xi_{t}|
in
(T)
below.

Assumption (T)
.

Let
X
satisfy
(A0)
, and let
(\tau,\lambda,\beta,\varepsilon,p)
satisfy
(A1)
. For the terminal condition
\xi
, we assume that there is a continuous adapted process
\Xi:[0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
\Xi_{T}=\xi
such that for any stopping time
S\leq T
,

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &|\Xi_{t}|\leq C_{1}(1+\|X\|^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}_{\infty;[0,t]}),\ \text{ for all }t\in[0,T]\text{ a.s.,}\\
&\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T}\right]\right\|^{k}_{p\text{-var};[S,T]}\right]\leq C_{1}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[|T-S|\right]\right\}^{l},\\
&\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Xi_{T}-\Xi_{S}\right|^{k}\right]\leq C_{1}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[|T-S|\right]\right\}^{l},\end{aligned}\right.

for some
k\in(1,2]
,
l>0
, and
C_{1}>0
independent of
S
.

Remark 2.4
.

When
N=1
, typical examples are given in Example
2.1
. In particular, the terminal value
\xi
is not necessarily bounded as assumed in
[
18
,
20
]
as well as in
[
48
, Assumption (H1)]
.

For simplicity of notation, we denote
\Theta_{2}:=(\tau,\lambda,\beta,p,k,l,\varepsilon,T,C_{1},C_{\text{Lip}},L,\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda;\beta}),
where all parameters
appear in assumptions
(H0)
,
(A0)
,
(A1)
, and
(T)
. These assumptions are made throughout Sections
2.1
and Section
2.2
, and without loss of generality, the proofs presented in this section assume
M=d=1
, unless otherwise specified.

2.1.
The existence

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume
X
satisfies Assumption
(A0)
. Then for any
p>2
and
q\geq 1,

(2.5)

m_{p,q}(X;[0,T])\lesssim_{p,q,L,T}1.

Proof.

Note that
X_{t}
has the representation
X_{t}=x+M_{t}+A_{t}
, where
M_{t}

is the diffusion and
A_{t}
is the drift. Then, since the quadratic variation process
\langle M\rangle
is bounded by
L^{2}T,
(
2.5
) follows directly from the BDG inequality for
p
-variation (see, e.g.,
[
48
, Lemma A.1]
).
∎

For
n\geq 1
, define the stopping time
T_{n}
as follows (we stipulate that
\inf\{\varnothing\}:=\infty
):

(2.6)

T_{n}:=T\land\inf\big\{t>0;\ |X_{t}|>n\big\}.

Lemma 2.2
.

Assume that
X
satisfies Assumption
(A0)
. Let
\{T_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of stopping times given by (
2.6
). Then for every
q>0
, there exists
C>0
such that for all
n\geq|x|,

(2.7)

\mathbb{E}\big[|T-T_{n}|^{q}\big]\leq C\exp\left\{-(n-|x|)^{2}/C\right\}.

Moreover, for any
h\in C^{\mathrm{Lip}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
and
q^{\prime}>1
, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that for all
n\geq|x|,

(2.8)

\mathbb{E}\left[|h(X_{T})-h(X_{T_{n}})|^{q^{\prime}}\right]\leq C^{\prime}\exp\left\{-(n-|x|)^{2}/C^{\prime}\right\}.

Proof.

By
[
49
, Corollary 4.1]
, with
(x_{t},\xi)
there replaced by
(X_{t}-x,0)
, there exists a constant
C^{*}>0
such that the following inequality holds for all
n\geq|x|,

(2.9)

\mathbb{P}\left\{T^{*}_{n-|x|}\leq T\right\}\leq 6\exp\left\{-(n-|x|)^{2}/C^{*}\right\},

where
T^{*}_{n-|x|}:=\inf\{t>0;|X_{t}-x|\geq n-|x|\}.
Noting that
\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}<T\}=\mathbb{P}\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X_{t}|>n\}
and
\mathbb{P}\{T^{*}_{n-|x|}\leq T\}=\mathbb{P}\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X_{t}-x|\geq n-|x|\},
by the triangular inequality
|X_{t}-x|+|x|\geq|X_{t}|
we have
\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}<T\}\leq\mathbb{P}\{T^{*}_{n-|x|}\leq T\}
. Then, from (
2.9
) it follows that

\mathbb{E}\big[|T-T_{n}|^{q}\big]\leq T^{q}\mathbb{P}\left\{T_{n}<T\right\}\leq T^{q}\mathbb{P}\left\{T^{*}_{n-|x|}\leq T\right\}\leq 6T^{q}\exp\left\{-(n-|x|)^{2}/C^{*}\right\},

and then (
2.7
) holds by taking
C:=(6T^{q})\vee C^{*}.

In addition, denoting by
C^{\prime}_{\text{Lip}}
the
Lipschitz constant of
h
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|h(X_{T})-h(X_{T_{n}})|^{q^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C^{\prime}_{\text{Lip}},q^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|X\|_{p\text{-var};[T_{n},T]}^{q^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{T_{n}}^{T}|\sigma(r,X_{r})|^{2}dr\Big|^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\bigg]+2\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{T_{n}}^{T}b(r,X_{r})dr\Big|^{q^{\prime}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[|T_{n}-T|^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[|T_{n}-T|^{q^{\prime}}\right],

where the second inequality is due to the BDG inequality for
p
-variation. Then (
2.8
) holds by (
2.7
).
∎

The following proposition shows that the limit of solutions on random intervals
[0,S_{n}]
is a solution on
[0,T]
when
S_{n}\uparrow T
as
n\to\infty.

Proposition 2.1
.

Assume
(H0)
and
(A0)
, and suppose that
(\beta,\varepsilon,\eta,g,f)
satisfies the conditions in
(A1)
except for
\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon\tau}<2
in (1).
Let
\left\{S_{n}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of stopping times increasing to
T
such that
X
is bounded on
[0,S_{n}]
(i.e.
\esssup_{(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega}|X_{t\land S_{n}}|<\infty
) for each
n\geq 1
. Assume that there is a sequence of processes
\{(Y^{n},Z^{n})\}_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathfrak{B}_{p,k^{\prime}}(0,T)
with some
k^{\prime}>1
, such that
\sup_{n\geq 1}\|Y^{n}_{S_{n}}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}<\infty
for some
q>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
. Further, assume that for each
n\geq 1
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\begin{cases}\displaystyle Y^{n}_{t\land S_{n}}=Y^{n}_{S_{n}}+\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}f(r,X_{r},Y^{n}_{r},Z^{n}_{r})dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}g_{i}(Y^{n}_{r})\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}Z^{n}_{r}dW_{r},\\[5.0pt]
Y^{n}_{t}=Y^{n}_{S_{n}},\ Z^{n}_{t}=0,\ \text{ for }\ t>S_{n},\end{cases}

and that there exists a continuous adapted process
Y
such that

(2.10)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y^{n}-Y\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right]=0.

Then, there exists a progressively measurable process
Z
such that
(Y,Z)\in\mathfrak{H}_{p,q}(0,T)
and satisfies (
1.1
) with
\xi=Y_{T}
.

If additionally
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[S_{n}=T\right]=1,
then we have

(2.11)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\|(Y^{n},Z^{n})-(Y,Z)\right\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q^{\prime}};[0,T]}=0,\text{ for every }q^{\prime}\in(1,q).

Proof.

Step 1.

In this step, we will prove the existence of
Z.
Denote

(2.12)

H:=\sup\limits_{m\geq 1}\|(Y^{m},Z^{m})\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q};[0,T]},

which is finite by the estimate obtained in
[
48
, Proposition 4.2]
,
with
f(\omega,t,y,z)
there replaced by
f(t,X_{t}(\omega),y,z)
. Denote
\delta Y^{n,m}_{r}:=Y^{n}_{r}-Y^{m}_{r}
,
\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}:=Z^{n}_{r}-Z^{m}_{r}
,
\delta f^{n,m}_{r}:=f(r,X_{r},Y^{n}_{r},Z^{n}_{r})-f(r,X_{r},Y^{m}_{r},Z^{m}_{r})
, and
\delta g^{n,m}_{r}:=g(Y^{n}_{r})-g(Y^{m}_{r})
. Split
[0,T]
into
[t_{i},t_{i+1}],

i=1,2,...,v,
evenly and denote
\theta:=\frac{T}{v}.
Let
\tau^{n}_{i}:=t_{i}\land S_{n}.
By the same calculation leading to

[
48
, (4.9)]
, for each
\kappa>1
and
i=1,2,...,v,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\delta Y^{n,m}\right\|^{\kappa}_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}\right]+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\Big|^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},\kappa}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}\delta g^{n,m}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}^{\kappa}\bigg]+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta f^{n,m}_{r}|dr\Big|^{\kappa}\bigg]+\mathbb{E}\left[|\delta Y^{n,m}_{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|^{\kappa}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},\kappa}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\left(1+\|X\|^{\lambda+\beta}_{\infty;[0,T]}+\|X\|^{\lambda+\beta}_{p\text{-var};[0,T]}\right)\left(1+\|Y^{n}\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}+\|Y^{m}\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}\right)^{1-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\times\left(\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}+\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right)^{1-\varepsilon}\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}^{\varepsilon}\Big|^{\kappa}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\theta^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\Big|^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}\bigg]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|^{\kappa}_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right],

where the second inequality follows from
[
48
, Lemma 2.1, Lemma 2.2]
,
and the fact
|\delta f^{n,m}_{r}|\lesssim|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|+|\delta Y^{n,m}_{r}|.

Since
q>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
, we can always choose an
\kappa>1
sufficiently close to
1
such that
q>\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon}.
Thus, for such
\kappa
we claim that the right-hand side of (
2.13
) is bounded by (up to a multiplicative constant)

(2.14)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}+\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right)^{\kappa}\Big]\right\}^{1-\varepsilon}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\left[\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}^{q}\right]\bigg\}^{\frac{\kappa\varepsilon}{q}}

\displaystyle+\theta^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\Big|^{\frac{\kappa}{2}}\bigg]+\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|^{\kappa}_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}.

The above claim can be verified by Hölder’s inequality as follows (noting
\frac{\varepsilon\kappa}{1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}}{\varepsilon}=\kappa<q
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|(AB^{1-\varepsilon})(C+D)^{1-\varepsilon}\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}^{\varepsilon}\Big|^{\kappa}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left|(AB^{1-\varepsilon})^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}}}\right|^{\kappa}\bigg]\bigg\}^{\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Big[(C+D)^{\frac{(1-\varepsilon)\kappa}{1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}}}\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}^{\frac{\varepsilon\kappa}{1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}}}\Big]\bigg\}^{1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x,H,\kappa,q}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Big[(C+D)^{\kappa}\Big]\bigg\}^{1-\varepsilon}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\Big[\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}^{q}\Big]\bigg\}^{\frac{\kappa\varepsilon}{q}},

where
A:=(1+\|X\|^{\lambda+\beta}_{\infty;[0,T]}+\|X\|^{\lambda+\beta}_{p\text{-var};[0,T]}),

B:=(1+\|Y^{n}\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}+\|Y^{m}\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}),

C:=\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]},

D:=\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]},

and the second inequality follows from
\mathbb{E}[|AB^{1-\varepsilon}|^{\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}}}]\leq\{\mathbb{E}[|A|^{\frac{q\kappa}{(q\varepsilon-\kappa)(1-\varepsilon)}}]\}^{\frac{q\varepsilon-\kappa}{q\varepsilon}}\{\mathbb{E}[|B|^{q}]\}^{\frac{\kappa}{q\varepsilon}}
(noting
\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon}<q
) and Assumption
(A0)
. For the first term of (
2.14
), by Young’s inequality, we have

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}+\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right)^{\kappa}\right]\right\}^{1-\varepsilon}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}^{q}\right]\right\}^{\frac{\kappa\varepsilon}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\theta^{\frac{\kappa}{1-\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}+\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right)^{\kappa}\right]+\theta^{-\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon}}\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|^{\kappa}_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

Then, combining (
2.13
), (
2.14
) and (
2.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left\|\delta Y^{n,m}\right\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+\Big\|\big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x,H,\kappa,q}\theta^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}\left\|\left\|\delta Y^{n,m}\right\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+\theta^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle+\theta^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|\big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+(1+\theta^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}})\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}.

Choosing
\theta>0
sufficiently small (the choice of
\theta
does not depend on
n
and
m
), we have

\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau^{n}_{i},\tau^{n}_{i+1}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+\Big\|\big|\int_{\tau^{n}_{i}}^{\tau^{n}_{i+1}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x,H,\kappa,q}\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

Noting that the above inequality holds for all
i=1,2,...,\frac{T}{\theta}
, we get

(2.16)

\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+\Big\|\big|\int_{0}^{S_{n}}|\delta Z^{n,m}_{r}|^{2}dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x,H,\kappa,q}\left\|\|\delta Y^{n,m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

On the other hand, by (
2.10
) and Fatou’s Lemma, we see that
\left\|\|Y\|_{\infty;[0,T]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
is finite. Therefore,
as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\left\|\|Y^{n}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S_{n}]}-Y\|_{\infty;[0,T]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\leq\left\|\|Y^{n}-Y\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}+\left\|\|Y\|_{\infty;[S_{n},T]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\longrightarrow 0,

which implies that
\{Y^{n}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S_{n}]}\}_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence with respect to the norm
\left\|\|\cdot\|_{\infty;[0,T]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
. In addition, by (
2.16
), we have that for
n<m^{\prime}<m
,

\displaystyle\left\|\|\delta Y^{m^{\prime},m}\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+\Big\|\big|\int_{0}^{S_{n}}|\delta Z^{m^{\prime},m}_{r}|^{2}dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x,H,\kappa,q}\left\|\|\delta Y^{m^{\prime},m}\|_{\infty;[0,S_{m^{\prime}}]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\|Y^{m^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S_{m^{\prime}}]}-Y^{m}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S_{m}]}\|_{\infty;[0,T]}\right\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

Hence,
\{Z^{m}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S_{n}]}(t)\}_{m\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence for each
n
, and we denote by
Z^{(n)}
the limit. Now let

Z_{t}:=Z^{(1)}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S_{1}]}(t)+\sum_{n\geq 2}Z^{(n)}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{(S_{n-1},S_{n}]}(t).

Then, for each
n\geq 1
,

(2.17)

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Big\{\left\|\left\|Y-Y^{m}\right\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}+\Big\|\big|\int_{0}^{S_{n}}|Z_{r}-Z^{m}_{r}|^{2}dr\big|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big\|_{L^{\kappa}(\Omega)}\Big\}=0.

Furthermore, by Fatou’s Lemma and the finiteness of
H,
we have
(Y,Z)\in\mathfrak{H}_{p,q}(0,T)
.

Step 2.

We will show that
(Y,Z)
is a solution. Note that for any
m>n
,

(2.18)

Y^{m}_{t\land S_{n}}=Y^{m}_{S_{n}}+\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}f(r,X_{r},Y^{m}_{r},Z^{m}_{r})dr+\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}g(Y^{m}_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}Z^{m}_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

By (
2.17
), (
2.10
), and
[
48
, Lemma 2.2]
, we have
\lim_{m}\|g(Y^{m})-g(Y)\|_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}=0
and
\lim_{m}\|g(Y^{m})-g(Y)\|_{\infty;[0,S_{n}]}=0
in probability.
Hence, by the estimate for Young integral in
[
48
, Proposition 2.1]
, letting
m
in (
2.18
) go to infinity, we obtain

Y_{t\land S_{n}}=Y_{S_{n}}+\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}f(r,X_{r},Y_{r},Z_{r})dr+\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}g(Y_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\land S_{n}}^{S_{n}}Z_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

Furthermore, letting
n
go to infinity we have

Y_{t}=Y_{T}+\int_{t}^{T}f(r,X_{r},Y_{r},Z_{r})dr+\int_{t}^{T}g(Y_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t}^{T}Z_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

Step 3.

Finally, we will prove (
2.11
) assuming
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[S_{n}=T\right]=1
. Note that
\big\{\|Y^{m}\|^{q^{\prime}}_{p\text{-var};[0,T]}\big\}_{m\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable, since
\sup_{m\geq 1}\|(Y^{m},Z^{m})\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q};[0,T]}<\infty
. Then, by (
2.17
) we have

\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y^{m}-Y\|^{q^{\prime}}_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}\right]=0\text{, for each }n\geq 1.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y^{m}-Y\right\|^{q^{\prime}}_{p\text{-var};[0,T]}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y^{m}-Y\right\|^{q^{\prime}}_{p\text{-var};[0,S_{n}]}\right]+\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{m\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y^{m}-Y\|^{q^{\prime}}_{p\text{-var};[0,T]}\mathbf{1}_{[S_{n}<T]}\right]=0,

where the second term on the right hand side converges to 0 due to the uniform integrability of
\|Y^{m}-Y\|^{q^{\prime}}_{p\text{-var};[0,T]}
and the assumption
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[S_{n}<T\right]=0
. Similarly, we can show
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[|\int_{0}^{T}|Z^{m}_{r}-Z_{r}|^{2}dr|^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{2}}]=0
, and this completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

In Proposition
2.1
, the boundedness of
g(\cdot)
is required, in order to ensure the uniform boundedness of
\|(Y^{m},Z^{m})\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q};[0,T]}
(see (
2.12
)). Indeed, to estimate the
L^{q}
-type norm as in
[
48
, Proposition 4.2]
, an interpolation argument is applied, which requires
g(\cdot)
to be bounded.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f:[0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a progressively measurable process, and let
X:[0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
g_{i}:[0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
i=1,2,...,M
, be continuous adapted processes. Assume that
(H0)
holds and
\eta\in C^{\tau,\lambda}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{M}).
Suppose that
\mathbb{P}(d\omega)
-a.s.
\|f\|_{L^{1}(0,T)}(\omega)<\infty,

\|X\|_{p\text{-}\mathrm{var};[0,T]}(\omega)<\infty,
and
\|g_{i}\|_{p\text{-}\mathrm{var};[0,T]}(\omega)<\infty
for
i=1,2,...,M
. Let
S\in[0,T]
be a stopping time, and
\xi\in\mathcal{F}_{S}
with
\|\xi\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\infty
. Assume
(Y,Z)\in\mathfrak{H}_{p,2}(0,T)
satisfies

Y_{t\land S}=\xi+\int_{t\land S}^{S}f_{r}dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{t\land S}^{S}g_{i}(r)\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\land S}^{S}Z_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

Then, for any
q>1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{S}Z_{r}dW_{r}\Big\|^{q}_{p\text{-}\mathrm{var};[t\land S,S]}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,q}\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{S}f_{r}dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{\cdot}^{S}g_{i}(r)\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{q}_{\infty;[t\land S,S]}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|\xi-\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\xi]\|^{q}_{\infty;[t\land S,S]}\right].

Proof.

By
[
48
, Lemma A.1]
with
M_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}Z_{r}\mathbf{1}_{[0,S]}(r)dW_{r},

(2.19)

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{S}Z_{r}dW_{r}\Big\|^{q}_{p\text{-var};[t\land S,S]}\right]\lesssim_{p,q}\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{S}Z_{r}dW_{r}\Big\|^{q}_{\infty;[t\land S,S]}\right].

Note that for all
t\in[0,T],
the following equations hold a.s.,

(2.20)

\int_{t\land S}^{S}Z_{r}dW_{r}=\xi-\mathbb{E}_{t\land S}[\xi]-(Y_{t\land S}-\mathbb{E}_{t\land S}[\xi])+\int_{t\land S}^{S}f_{r}dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{t\land S}^{S}g_{i}(r)\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r}),

and

(2.21)

Y_{t\land S}-\mathbb{E}_{t\land S}\left[\xi\right]=\mathbb{E}_{t\land S}\left[\int_{t\land S}^{S}f_{r}dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{t\land S}^{S}g_{i}(r)\eta(dr,X_{r})\right].

By (
2.21
) and Doob’s maximal inequality, we get for
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\left\|Y_{\cdot}-\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\xi\right]\right\|^{q}_{\infty;[t\land S,S]}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\left|\mathbb{E}_{s}\bigg[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{S}f_{r}dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{\cdot}^{S}g_{i}(r)\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})\Big\|_{\infty;[t\land S,S]}\bigg]\right|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{q}\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{S}f_{r}dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{\cdot}^{S}g_{i}(r)\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{q}_{\infty;[t\land S,S]}\right].

This together with (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) gives the desired estimate.
∎

To apply the localization argument, we need a uniform estimate for the localized solutions, which is provided by the following lemma. For each
n\geq 1
, consider the following equation:

(2.22)

\begin{cases}dY^{n}_{t}=-f(t,X_{t},Y^{n}_{t},Z^{n}_{t})dt-\sum_{i=1}^{M}g_{i}(Y^{n}_{t})\eta_{i}(dt,X_{t})+Z^{n}_{t}dW_{t},\quad t\in[0,T_{n}],\\
Y^{n}_{T_{n}}=\Xi_{T_{n}},\end{cases}

where
T_{n}
is defined in (
2.6
) and
\Xi_{\cdot}
satisfies
(T)
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume
(A0)
,
(T)
, and
(A1)
. Let
(Y^{n},Z^{n})\in\bigcap_{q>1}\mathfrak{B}_{p,q}(0,T)
be the unique solution of Eq. (
2.22
) (see
[
48
, Section 4]
for its well-posedness). Then for every
q>1,

(2.23)

m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[0,T])+\|Z^{n}\|_{q\text{-}\mathrm{BMO};[0,T]}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}\ \text{ for all }\ n\geq|x|.

Proof.

For
r\in[0,T]
, denote
f^{n}_{r}:=f(r,X_{r},Y^{n}_{r},Z^{n}_{r})
. By (
2.2
) in
(A1)
, we have

(2.24)

\displaystyle\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\Big|\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}|f^{n}_{r}|dr\Big|^{q}\Big]\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}|T-t|\left(1+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}\right)+C_{\text{Lip}}|T-t|\left\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|Y^{n}\|^{q}_{\infty;[t,T]}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{q}}+C_{\text{Lip}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\Big|\int_{t}^{T}|Z^{n}_{r}|dr\Big|^{q}\Big]\Big\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}+\|Y^{n}_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+|T-t|m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])+|T-t|^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|Z^{n}\right\|_{q\text{-BMO};[t,T]},

where in the second step we use the fact

\|Y^{n}\|_{\infty;[r,T]}\leq\|Y^{n}\|_{p\text{-var};[r,T]}+|Y_{T_{n}}^{n}|.
For every
t\in[0,T]
and
n\geq|x|
, by the estimate for nonlinear Young integral established in
[
48
, Lemma 2.1]
, we have

(2.25)

\displaystyle\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}g(Y^{n}_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{q}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n+1},T_{n+1}]}\right]\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}T^{\tau}\|\eta\|_{\tau,\lambda;\beta}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\left(1+\|X\|^{q\beta}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right)\|g(Y^{n}_{\cdot})\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\|X\|^{q\lambda}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}

\displaystyle+\left(1+\|X\|^{q(\lambda+\beta)}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right)\Big(\|g(Y^{n}_{\cdot})\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}+\|g(Y^{n}_{\cdot})\|^{q\varepsilon}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\|g(Y^{n}_{\cdot})\|^{q(1-\varepsilon)}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\Big)\Big]\Big\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}\left(1+n^{\beta}\right)+\left(1+n^{\lambda+\beta}\right)\left(1+m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])^{1-\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\lambda+\beta}\left(1+m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])^{1-\varepsilon}\right),

where in the second step, we use the fact
\|X\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\leq n,
the finiteness of
m_{p,q}(X;[0,T])
obtained in Lemma
2.1
, and the finiteness and Lipschitzness of
g(\cdot).

By Lemma
2.3
and the following equation

Y^{n}_{t}=\Xi_{T_{n}}+\int_{0}^{t\land T_{n}}f^{n}_{r}dr+\int_{0}^{t\land T_{n}}g(Y^{n}_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})-\int_{0}^{t\land T_{n}}Z^{n}_{r}dW_{r},

we get

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|Y^{n}\|^{q}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{q}}+\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}Z^{n}_{r}dW_{r}\Big\|^{q}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}\|Y^{n}_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}g(Y^{n}_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{q}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}+\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big|\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}|f^{n}_{r}|dr\Big|^{q}\right]\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+n^{\lambda+\beta}m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])^{1-\varepsilon}+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}+|T-t|m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])

\displaystyle+|T-t|^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|Z^{n}\right\|_{q\text{-BMO};[t,T]},

where the second inequality follows from (
2.24
)
and (
2.25
). Then, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(2.26)

\displaystyle m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])+\left\|Z^{n}\right\|_{q\text{-BMO};[t,T]}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+n^{\lambda+\beta}m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])^{1-\varepsilon}+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}+|T-t|m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])

\displaystyle+|T-t|^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|Z^{n}\right\|_{q\text{-BMO};[t,T]}\Big).

Note that by Young’s inequality,

n^{\lambda+\beta}m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])^{1-\varepsilon}\leq(3C)^{\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}}+\frac{1}{3C}m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T]).

Thus, we can choose
\theta:=T-t
sufficiently small so that
C(\theta^{\frac{1}{2}}\vee\theta)\leq\frac{1}{3},and

then (
2.26
) yields

(2.27)

m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[t,T])+\|Z^{n}\|_{q\text{-BMO};[t,T]}\\
\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}.

Similarly, setting
T^{n}_{i}:=\left(T-(i-1)\theta\right)\vee 0,
we have for
i=1,2,...,\lfloor\frac{T}{\theta}\rfloor+1,

(2.28)

m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[T^{n}_{i+1},T^{n}_{i}])+\|Z^{n}\|_{q\text{-BMO};[T^{n}_{i+1},T^{n}_{i}]}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}\|Y^{n}_{T_{n}\land T^{n}_{i}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}.

From (
1.5
) it follows that

(2.29)

\|Y^{n}_{T_{n}\land T^{n}_{i}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\sum_{2\leq j\leq i}m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[T^{n}_{j},T^{n}_{j-1}])+\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}.

By iterating (
2.28
) and (
2.29
), and by the estimate (
2.27
), we have

\displaystyle m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[T^{n}_{i+1},T^{n}_{i}])+\|Z^{n}\|_{q\text{-BMO};[T^{n}_{i+1},T^{n}_{i}]}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[T^{n}_{2},T])+\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}.

In addition, by the bound
\|\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{1}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}
which follows from Assumption
(T)
, we have

m_{p,q}(Y^{n};[T^{n}_{i+1},T^{n}_{i}])+\|Z^{n}\|_{q\text{-BMO};[T^{n}_{i+1},T^{n}_{i}]}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},q}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}.

Summing up the above inequalities for
i=1,2,...,\lfloor\frac{T}{\theta}\rfloor+1
, we obtain the desired (
2.23
).
∎

The existence of the solution to Eq. (
1.1
) is obtained in Theorem
1
below.

Theorem 1
.

Assume
(A0)
,
(T)
, and
(A1)
. Then, Eq. (
1.1
) admits a solution
(Y,Z)\in\bigcap_{q>1}\mathfrak{H}_{p,q}(0,T)
. In addition, for
n\geq 1
, let
T_{n}
be the stopping time defined in (
2.6
) and
(Y^{n},Z^{n})\in\bigcap_{q>1}\mathfrak{B}_{p,q}(0,T)
be the unique solution of Eq. (
2.22
) (see
[
48
, Section 4]
for its well-posedness). Then we have for each
q>1
,

(2.30)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|(Y^{n},Z^{n})-(Y,Z)\right\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q};[0,T]}=0.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

We provide a detailed proof for the case
N=1
and
f\equiv 0
here. The reader is referred to Appendix
C
for a complete proof which basically follows the strategy presented here while is more involved.

Note that
[
48
, Assumption (H2)]
with
S_{0}=T_{n}
holds for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By the well-posedness of BSDEs with stopping terminal times established in
[
48
, Proposition 4.1]
, we have
\|(Y^{n},Z^{n})\|_{p,q;[0,T]}<\infty
for every
n\geq 1
and
q>1
. For
n\geq 1

denote

(2.31)

\delta Y^{n}_{t}:=Y^{n+1}_{t}-Y^{n}_{t},\ \delta g^{n}_{t}:=g(Y^{n+1}_{t})-g(Y^{n}_{t}),\text{ and }\alpha^{n}_{t}:=\frac{\delta g^{n}_{t}}{\delta Y^{n}_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\delta Y^{n}_{t}\neq 0\}}.

It follows that

(2.32)

\begin{cases}d\delta Y^{n}_{t}=-\alpha^{n}_{t}\delta Y^{n}_{t}\eta(dt,X_{t})+\delta Z^{n}_{t}dW_{t},\ t\in[0,T_{n}],\\
\delta Y^{n}_{T_{n}}=Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}.\end{cases}

Our goal is to show that
\{Y^{n}=Y^{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\delta Y^{i},n\geq 2\}
is a Cauchy sequence in a proper Banach space, and for this purpose, we shall estimate
\delta Y^{n}
. This will be done in two steps.

Step 1.

Let
A^{t;n}_{s}
be the unique solution of the following Young differential equation

(2.33)

A^{t;n}_{s}=1+\int_{t}^{s}\alpha^{n}_{r}A^{t;n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r}),\quad s\in[t,T].

In this step, we will estimate the conditional
\frac{k}{k-1}
-moments of
A^{t\land T_{n};n}_{T_{n}}

(recall that
k\in(1,2]
is a given constant in Assumption
(T)
). This estimate will be used in the next step to provide an estimate for
\delta Y^{n}_{t},
in view of the representation
\delta Y^{n}_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{t\land T_{n}}[A^{t\land T_{n};n}_{T_{n}}\cdot\delta Y^{n}_{T_{n}}].

Clearly, we have
\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T],\omega\in\Omega}|\alpha^{n}_{t}|\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}1
. In addition, since
\nabla g
is bounded, we have (following the calculus as in
[
48
, (3.41)]
)

\|\alpha^{n}\|_{p\text{-var};[t,T]}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\left(\|Y^{n}\|_{p\text{-var};[t,T]}+\|Y^{n+1}\|_{p\text{-var};[t,T]}\right).

Then, by the above inequality and (
2.23
), we have

(2.34)

m_{p,2}(\alpha^{n};[0,T])\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}\text{ for all }n\geq|x|.

Denote

\Gamma^{n}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{t\land T_{n}}\left[|A^{t\land T_{n};n}_{T_{n}}|^{\frac{k}{k-1}}\right].

Since
A^{t;n}_{s}=\exp\{\int_{t}^{s}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\},
we have

(2.35)

\Gamma^{n}_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\exp\Big\{\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\frac{k}{k-1}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\}\right].

By the well-posedness for linear BSDEs with bounded drivers
[
48
, Corollary 3.3]
, there exists a process
\gamma^{n}
such that
(\Gamma^{n},\gamma^{n})
satisfies the following linear BSDE

1
1

1

When
N>1,
the representation (
2.35
) fails in general. However, following the same line of proof as in
[
48
, Proposition 3.5]
, we can show that
\Gamma^{n}_{t}
is bounded by the solution of some linear BSDE with terminal time
T_{n}
. Consequently, the estimate for
\Gamma^{n}
will be the same as for
N=1
. See Remark
2.6
and
Appendix C
The proof of Theorem
for the general case
of Theorem
1
for further discussion.
,

(2.36)

\begin{cases}d\Gamma^{n}_{t}=-\frac{k}{k-1}\alpha^{n}_{t}\Gamma^{n}_{t}\eta(dt,X_{t})+\gamma^{n}_{t}dW_{t},\ t\in[0,T_{n}],\\
\Gamma^{n}_{T_{n}}=1.\end{cases}

Moreover, by
[
48
, Proposition 3.3 and Remark 4.1]
, we have

\|(\Gamma^{n},\gamma^{n})\|_{p,2;[0,T]}<\infty
, which implies that
\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T],\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Gamma^{n}_{t}\right|<\infty.
Then apply the estimate for nonlinear Young integrals
[
48
, Proposition 2.1]
to get that for
t\in[0,T]
,

(2.37)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}\alpha^{n}_{r}\Gamma^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}|T-t|^{2\tau}\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\Big(\|\alpha^{n}\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{\infty;[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}+\|\alpha^{n}\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\Big)\left(1+\|X\|^{2(\lambda+\beta)}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right)

\displaystyle+\|\alpha^{n}\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{\infty;[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\|X\|^{2\lambda}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\left(1+\|X\|^{2\beta}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right)\Big].

We estimate each term on the right-hand side of the above inequality. In view of the fact
\Gamma_{T_{n}}^{n}=1,

(2.38)

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|\alpha^{n}\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{\infty;[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\esssup_{s\in[t,T],\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Gamma^{n}_{s}\right|^{2}\lesssim 1+m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])^{2}.

Furthermore, by the above inequality, we have

(2.39)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|\alpha^{n}\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\\
&\lesssim\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\esssup_{s\in[t,T],\omega\in\Omega}\left|\alpha^{n}_{s}\right|^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|\alpha^{n}\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\esssup_{s\in[t,T],\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Gamma^{n}_{s}\right|^{2}\\
&\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])^{2}+m_{p,2}(\alpha^{n};[t,T])^{2}\left(1+m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])^{2}\right).\end{split}

In addition, by Lemma
2.1
, we have

(2.40)

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\|\alpha^{n}\Gamma^{n}\|^{2}_{\infty;[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\|X\|^{2\lambda}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\esssup_{s\in[t,T],\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Gamma^{n}_{s}\right|^{2}\cdot m_{p,2}(X;[0,T])^{2\lambda}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}1+m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])^{2}.

In view of (
2.37
), (
2.38
), (
2.39
), (
2.40
), and the fact that
\|X\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\leq n
for
n\geq|x|
, we obtain

(2.41)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{t}\Big[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}\alpha^{n}_{r}\Gamma^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\Big]\\
&\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}|T-t|^{2\tau}n^{2(\lambda+\beta)}\left(1+m_{p,2}(\alpha^{n};[0,T])^{2}\right)\left(1+m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])^{2}\right).\end{split}

On the other hand, in view of (
2.36
) and Lemma
2.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}\gamma^{n}_{r}dW_{r}\Big\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}\alpha^{n}_{r}\Gamma^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right],

which, by (
2.41
), implies

(2.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\Big\|\int_{\cdot}^{T_{n}}\gamma^{n}_{r}dW_{r}\Big\|^{2}_{p\text{-var};[t\land T_{n},T_{n}]}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}|T-t|^{2\tau}n^{2(\lambda+\beta)}\left(1+m_{p,2}(\alpha^{n};[0,T])^{2}\right)\left(1+m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])^{2}\right).

Combining (
2.36
), (
2.41
), (
2.42
), and noting
m_{p,2}(\alpha^{n};[0,T])\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}n^{\frac{\lambda+\beta}{\varepsilon}\vee 1}
, there exists
C>0
such that for every
t\in[0,T]
and
n\geq|x|
,

(2.43)

m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])\leq C|T-t|^{\tau}\left(n^{\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon}\vee(\lambda+\beta+1)}+n^{\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\varepsilon}\vee(\lambda+\beta+1)}m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])\right).

Thus, for any
t\in[0,T]
satisfying
|T-t|\leq\delta
with

(2.44)

\delta:=(2C)^{-\frac{1}{\tau}}n^{-(\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\tau\varepsilon}\vee\frac{\lambda+\beta+1}{\tau})},

the inequality (
2.43
) yields that
m_{p,2}(\Gamma^{n};[t,T])\leq 1
, which then, according to (
1.5
), implies the following local estimate

(2.45)

\esssup_{s\in[T-\delta,T],\omega\in\Omega}|\Gamma^{n}_{s}|\leq 2.

To obtain a global estimate, for
0\leq j\leq\left\lfloor T/\delta\right\rfloor
, denote by
T^{j}_{n}:=T_{n}\land(T-j\delta),
and
(\Gamma^{n;j}_{t},\gamma^{n;j}_{t})
the unique solution of

\begin{cases}d\Gamma^{n;j}_{s}=-\alpha^{n}_{s}\Gamma^{n;j}_{s}\eta(ds,X_{s})+\gamma^{n;j}_{s}dW_{s},\ s\in[0,T^{j}_{n}],\\
\Gamma^{n;j}_{T^{j}_{n}}=1.\end{cases}

Similar to (
2.45
), we have (let
T-(j+1)\delta=0
if
(j+1)\delta>T
)

(2.46)

\esssup_{s\in[T-(j+1)\delta,T-j\delta],\omega\in\Omega}\left|\Gamma^{n;j}_{s}\right|\leq 2.

Denoting
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{t;n}_{s}:=|A^{t;n}_{s}|^{\frac{k}{k-1}},
we have
\Gamma^{n;j}_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\widetilde{\Gamma}^{t\land T^{j}_{n};n}_{T^{j}_{n}}\right]
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{t;n}_{s}=\exp\left\{\int_{t}^{s}\frac{k}{k-1}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\right\}.

By (
2.46
) and the equality
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{T^{j+1}_{n};n}_{T^{j}_{n}}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{T^{j+2}_{n};n}_{T^{j+1}_{n}}=\widetilde{\Gamma}^{T^{j+2}_{n};n}_{T^{j}_{n}},
we have

\left|\Gamma^{n;j}_{T^{j+2}_{n}}\right|=\left|\mathbb{E}_{T^{j+2}_{n}}\left[\widetilde{\Gamma}^{T^{j+2}_{n};n}_{T^{j}_{n}}\right]\right|=\mathbb{E}_{T^{j+2}_{n}}\left[\widetilde{\Gamma}^{T^{j+2;n}_{n}}_{T^{j+1}_{n}}\mathbb{E}_{T^{j+1}_{n}}\left[\widetilde{\Gamma}^{T^{j+1}_{n};n}_{T^{j}_{n}}\right]\right]\leq 4,

and then

(2.47)

\esssup_{s\in[0,T],\omega\in\Omega}|\Gamma^{n}_{s}|\leq\prod_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor T/\delta\right\rfloor}\sup_{t\in[T-(j+1)\delta,T-j\delta]}\left\|\mathbb{E}_{t\land T^{j}_{n}}\left[\widetilde{\Gamma}^{t\land T^{j}_{n};n}_{T^{j}_{n}}\right]\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 2^{\frac{T}{\delta}+1}.

Thus, in view of (
2.44
) and (
2.47
), there exists a constant
C^{*}>0
such that

(2.48)

\esssup_{s\in[0,T],\omega\in\Omega}|\Gamma^{n}_{s}|\leq\exp\left\{C^{*}n^{\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\tau\varepsilon}\vee\frac{\lambda+\beta+1}{\tau}}\right\},\text{ for all }n\geq|x|.

Step 2.
We are ready to estimate
\delta Y^{n}=Y^{n+1}-Y^{n}
which satisfies the linear BSDE (
2.32
). By the product rule shown in
[
48
, Corollary 2.1]
, we have

\delta Y^{n}_{t\land T_{n}}=A^{t\land T_{n};n}_{T_{n}}\left(Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\right)-\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}A^{t\land T_{n};n}_{r}\delta Z^{n}_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

Taking conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{t\land T_{n}}[\cdot]
on both sides of the above equation leads to

(2.49)

\|\delta Y^{n}_{\cdot}\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}=\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot\land T_{n}}\left[A^{\cdot\land T_{n};n}_{T_{n}}\left(Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\right)\right]\right\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}.

From the regularity results obtained in
[
48
, Proposition 4.3]
, it follows that

(2.50)

\displaystyle\|Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n+1}}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}+\|\Xi_{T_{n+1}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x}\left\||T_{n+1}-T_{n}|^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{4k}(\Omega)}+\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T_{n+1}}\right]\|_{p\text{-var};[T_{n},T_{n+1}]}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}+\|\Xi_{T_{n+1}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}.

In addition, by the triangular inequality, the BDG inequality for
p
-variation, and condition (
2.4
), we have

(2.51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T_{n+1}}\right]\|^{k}_{p\text{-var};[T_{n},T_{n+1}]}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T_{n+1}}-\Xi_{T}\right]\|^{k}_{p\text{-var};[T_{n},T_{n+1}]}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T}\right]\|^{k}_{p\text{-var};[T_{n},T_{n+1}]}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Xi_{T_{n+1}}-\Xi_{T}|^{k}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T}\right]\|^{k}_{p\text{-var};[T_{n},T_{n+1}]}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[|T-T_{n}|\right]\right\}^{l}.

Thus, combining (
2.50
), (
2.51
), and Lemma
2.2
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that

(2.52)

\|Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}n^{2}\right\}.

Recall
\Gamma_{t}^{n}=\mathbb{E}_{t\land T_{n}}\left[|A^{t\land T_{n};n}_{T_{n}}|^{\frac{k}{k-1}}\right]
.
Applying Hölder’s inequality to (
2.49
), by (
2.48
) and (
2.52
), we have

(2.53)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\delta Y^{n}_{\cdot}\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Big\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\mathbb{E}_{t\land T_{n}}\left[\left|Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\right|^{k}\right]\right|\Big\}^{\frac{1}{k}}\Big\{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Gamma^{n}_{t}\right|\Big\}^{\frac{k-1}{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\|Y^{n+1}_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{k}(\Omega)}\Big\{\esssup_{t\in[0,T],\omega\in\Omega}|\Gamma^{n}_{t}|\Big\}^{\frac{k-1}{k}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},x}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}n^{2}+\frac{(k-1)C^{*}}{k}n^{\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\tau\varepsilon}\vee\frac{\lambda+\beta+1}{\tau}}\right\}.

By Assumption
(A1)
and Remark
2.3
,
\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(\lambda+\beta)}{\tau\varepsilon}\vee\frac{\lambda+\beta+1}{\tau}<2
. This fact, combined with (
2.53
), implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m\geq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\delta Y^{m}\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right]=0.

Hence, for each fixed
n\geq 1,
we have

(2.54)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\lim_{n^{\prime}\to\infty}\sum_{m\geq n^{\prime}}\|\delta Y^{m}\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\Big]=0.

Let
Y_{t}:=\limsup_{m\to\infty}Y^{m}_{t}
. Then
Y
is adapted, and by (
2.54
),
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|Y^{m}-Y\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}=0
a.s. for each
n\geq 1
. Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\{T_{n}=T\}=1
due to the fact that
\left[T_{n}<T\right]=[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|X_{t}|>n]
, it follows that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\|Y^{m}-Y\|_{\infty;[0,T]}=0
a.s. Thus, the adapted process
Y
is a.s. continuous on
[0,T].
Furthermore, by Fatou’s Lemma, we have that

(2.55)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y^{n}-Y\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right]\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m\geq n}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\delta Y^{m}\|_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right]=0.

According to
[
48
, Proposition 4.2]
, we have for every
q>1
,

\sup_{n}\|(Y^{n},Z^{n})\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q+\varepsilon};[0,T]}<\infty.

Then, for all
q>1
,
\mathbb{E}[\|Y^{n}\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}]
is uniformly bounded in
n
, and hence
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Y\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,T]}\big]<\infty
by Fatou’s Lemma. Since
\|Y^{n}-Y\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}
is uniformly integrable in
n
, from (
2.55
) it follows that, for all
q>1
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y^{n}-Y\|^{q}_{\infty;[0,T_{n}]}\right]=0.

Thus, by Proposition
2.1
(with
S_{n}=T_{n}
), there exists a process
Z
such that
(Y,Z)
is a solution of (
1.1
) with
(Y,Z)\in\mathfrak{H}_{p,q}(0,T).
Furthermore, noting that
\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[T_{n}=T\right]=1
, by Proposition
2.1
again, we have

(2.56)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left\|(Y^{n},Z^{n})-(Y,Z)\right\|_{\mathfrak{H}_{p,q^{\prime}};[0,T]}=0,\text{ for every }q^{\prime}\in\left(1,q\right).

This yields the desired (
2.30
), since
q
can be arbitrarily large. The proof is concluded.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

In the above proof, when proving the key estimate (
2.53
), one critical step is to estimate
\Gamma^{n}_{t}
, i.e., to obtain (
2.48
). This is achieved, in Step 1 of the proof, by analyzing BSDE (
2.36
) in stead of estimating directly the explicit expression (
2.35
) for the following reasons:

(1)

When
N>1
, in general
\Gamma^{n}_{t}
does not admit an explicit expression (see Remark
2.9
), while the estimates (
2.41
)–(
2.48
) of BSDE (
2.36
) can be extended to the multi-dimensional case in a straightforward way (see (
C.9
)–(
C.18
) in
Appendix C
The proof of Theorem
for the general case
).

(2)

Even for the case
N=1
, the expression (
2.35
)

\Gamma_{t}^{n}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\exp\left\{\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\frac{k}{k-1}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\right\}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\left|\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\frac{k}{k-1}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\right|^{j}\right]

does not make the analysis easier due to the nonlinear Young integral. More precisely, letting
\Lambda_{t}:=\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\frac{k}{k-1}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})
and following the argument used in
[
20
, p. 12]
, although we still have

\left|\Lambda_{t}\right|^{j+1}=(j+1)\int_{t\land T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\frac{k}{k-1}\left|\Lambda_{r}\right|^{j}\alpha^{n}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r}),

the extra term
X_{r}
makes the growth of
j\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{t}[\left|\Lambda_{t}\right|^{j+1}]
much more involved (see
[
48
, (2.1)]
with
(y_{r},x_{r})
replaced by
(|\Lambda_{r}|^{j}\alpha^{n}_{r},X_{r})
).

Example 2.1
.

We provide some examples of
\Xi=\{\Xi_{t},t\in[0,T]\}
which satisfies Assumption
(T)
with
k=2
. Let
(\tau,\lambda,\beta,\varepsilon,p)
be parameters given in
(A1)
and suppose that
X
satisfies Assumption
(A0)
.
Assume
\Xi_{T}\in\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
and

(2.57)

\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[|D_{t}(\Xi_{T})|^{q}\right]<\infty,\text{ for some }q>2,

where
D
is the Malliavin derivative. By Clark-Ocone’s formula (see Clark
[
12
]
or Nualart
[
43
, Proposition 1.3.14]
), the BDG inequality (see
[
48
, Corollary A.1]
), and Hölder’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\Xi_{T}\right]\right\|^{2}_{p\text{-}\mathrm{var};[S,T]}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{S}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[D_{t}(\Xi_{T})\right]\right|^{2}dt\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[|D_{t}(\Xi_{T})|^{q}\right]\right\}^{\frac{2}{q}}\cdot T^{\frac{2}{q}}\cdot\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[|T-S|\right]\right\}^{\frac{q-2}{q}}.

Assume further that for all
t\in[0,T]

(2.58)

|\Xi_{t}|\lesssim\left(1+\|X\|_{\infty;[0,t]}\right),\ \ \mathbb{E}\left[|\Xi_{S}-\Xi_{T}|^{2}\right]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[|T-S|\right],

for all stopping time
S\leq T
. Then,
\Xi
satisfies Assumption
(T)
with
l=\frac{q-2}{q}
. Below we provide three examples of
\Xi
satisfying (
2.57
) and (
2.58
).

(a)

Let
\Xi_{t}:=h(X_{t_{1}\land t},X_{t_{2}\land t},...,X_{t_{m}\land t}),
where
h\in C^{\mathrm{Lip}}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times m};\mathbb{R}).
Now we briefly justify the two conditions (
2.57
) and (
2.58
). First, we refer to
[
43
, Theorem 2.2.1]
for the following fact,

(2.59)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|D_{t}(X_{s})|^{q^{\prime}}\Big]\lesssim 1,\ \text{for every}\ q^{\prime}\geq 1.

Noting this inequality and the Lipschitzness of
h
, by
[
43
, Proposition 1.2.4]
we have
|D_{t}(\Xi_{T})|\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{m}|D_{t}(X_{t_{j}})|
,
and hence (
2.57
) follows from (
2.59
) for all
q>2
. Second, the representation (
2.1
) and the boundedness of
(\sigma,b)
imply (
2.58
).

(b)

Let
\Xi_{t}:=h(W_{\cdot\land t})
where
h\in C^{\mathrm{Lip}}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d});\mathbb{R})
with
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
equipped with the uniform metric. According to Cheridito-Nam
[
11
, Proposition 3.2]
, the Malliavin derivative of
\Xi_{T}=h(W_{\cdot})
is bounded, and therefore (
2.57
) holds for every
q>2.
In addition, (
2.58
) is satisfied, since by the BDG inequality for
p
-variation (see, e.g.,
[
48
, Corollary A.1]
) we have

\mathbb{E}[|\Xi_{S}-\Xi_{T}|^{2}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\|W_{\cdot\vee S}-W_{S}\|^{2}_{\infty;[0,T]}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\|W_{\cdot}\|^{2}_{p\text{-}\mathrm{var};[S,T]}]\lesssim_{p}\mathbb{E}[|T-S|].

(c)

Let
\Xi_{t}:=\sup_{s\leq t}X^{1}_{s}
, where
X^{1}
is the first component of
X
. Clearly we have
|\Xi_{t}|\leq\|X\|_{\infty;[0,t]}
and

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Xi_{S}-\Xi_{T}\right|^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|X\right\|^{2}_{p\text{-}\mathrm{var};[S,T]}\right]\lesssim_{L,p,T}\mathbb{E}\left[|T-S|\right],

where the last inequality is from the BDG inequality for
p
-variation. The condition (
2.58
) is verified. For the condition (
2.57
), it can be proved by adapting the proof of
[
43
, Proposition 2.1.10]
.

Proposition
2.2
below indicates that the solution of (
1.1
) is independent of the approximating stopping times
\{T_{n}\}_{n}
given in (
2.6
), i.e., one can replace
\{T_{n}\}_{n}
by any other sequence of stopping times
\{S_{n}\}_{n}
increasing to
T
such that
X
is bounded on
[0,S_{n}]
. This convergence result will play a key role in the study of Young PDEs in Section
3.1
.

Let
S
be a stopping time such that
S\leq T
and
X
is bounded on
[0,S]
. By
[
48
, Proposition 4.1]
, there exists
(Y^{S},Z^{S})\in\mathfrak{B}_{p,2}(0,T)
uniquely solving BSDE

(2.60)

Y_{t\wedge S}=\Xi_{S}+\int_{t\land S}^{S}f(r,X_{r},Y_{r},Z_{r})dr+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\int_{t\wedge S}^{S}g_{i}(Y_{r})\eta_{i}(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\wedge S}^{S}Z_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

Proposition 2.2
.

Assume
(A0)
,
(T)
, and
(A1)
are satisfied. We also assume
|x|\leq C_{X}
for some constant
C_{X}>0
where we recall
X_{0}=x
by (
2.1
). Let
\left\{S_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
be a sequence of stopping times increasing to
T
such that
X
is bounded on each
[0,S_{j}]
. Let
(Y^{S_{j}},Z^{S_{j}})
be the unique solution to (
2.60
) with
S=S_{j}
, and let
(Y,Z)
be the solution to (
1.1
) by Theorem
1
. Then, for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\{Y^{S_{j}}_{S_{j}\land t}\}_{j\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, converging to
Y_{t}
.

If we further assume
\mathbb{P}\{S_{j}\neq T\}\leq p_{j}
for some sequence
\{p_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
with
p_{j}\downarrow 0
, then for every
\delta>0
, there exists
J_{\delta}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
j\geq J_{\delta},

(2.61)

\|Y^{S_{j}}_{S_{j}\land t}-Y_{t}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\leq\delta,

where
J_{\delta}
only depends on
\delta
,
C_{X}
,
\{p_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
, and
\Theta_{2}.

Proof.

We shall prove for the case
k=2,

f\equiv 0,
and
N=1
. The general case can be proved in a similar way as in
Appendix C
The proof of Theorem
for the general case
of Theorem
1
.

Step 1.
We first prove (
2.61
), assuming
|x|\leq C_{X}
and
\mathbb{P}\{S_{j}\neq T\}\leq p_{j}
with
p_{j}\downarrow 0
. Let
T_{n}
be defined as in (
2.6
), and
Y^{n}
be the unique solution of (
2.22
).
Let
(Y^{n,j},Z^{n,j}):=(Y^{\tau_{n,j}},Z^{\tau_{n,j}})\in\mathfrak{B}_{p,2}(0,T)
be the unique solution of (
2.60
) with
S=\tau_{n,j}:=T_{n}\wedge S_{j}
for
n,j\geq 1
. Hence, we have

Y_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}^{n,j}=\Xi_{\tau_{n,j}}+\int_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}^{\tau_{n,j}}g(Y^{n,j}_{r})\eta(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}^{\tau_{n,j}}Z^{n,j}_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T].

The difference between
(Y^{n},Z^{n})
and
(Y^{n,j},Z^{n,j})
satisfies the following linear BSDE:

\delta Y^{n,j}_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}=Y^{n}_{\tau_{n,j}}-\Xi_{\tau_{n,j}}+\int_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}^{\tau_{n,j}}\alpha^{n,j}_{r}\delta Y^{n,j}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})-\int_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}^{\tau_{n,j}}\delta Z^{n,j}_{r}dW_{r},\ t\in[0,T],

where
\delta Y^{n,j}_{t}:=Y^{n}_{t}-Y^{n,j}_{t}
,
\delta Z^{n}_{t}:=Z^{n}_{t}-Z^{n,j}_{t}
, and
\alpha^{n,j}_{t}:=\frac{g(Y^{n}_{t})-g(Y^{n,j}_{t})}{\delta Y^{n,j}_{t}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\delta Y^{n,j}_{t}=0\}}(t)
, and thus we have the Feynman-Kac formula:

(2.62)

\delta Y^{n,j}_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}=\mathbb{E}_{t\land\tau_{n,j}}\bigg[\left(Y^{n}_{\tau_{n,j}}-\Xi_{\tau_{n,j}}\right)\exp\Big\{\int_{t\land\tau_{n,j}}^{\tau_{n,j}}\alpha^{n,j}_{r}\eta(dr,X_{r})\Big\}\bigg],\ t\in[0,T].

We shall estimate
\|Y_{S_{j}\wedge t}^{S_{j}}-Y_{t}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
in three steps.

(i)
In the first step, we estimate
\|\delta Y^{n,j}_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=\|Y^{n}_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}-Y^{n,j}_{t\wedge\tau_{n,j}}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
.
In view of (
2.62
), we first estimate
\|Y^{n}_{\tau_{n,j}}-\Xi_{\tau_{n,j}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
. By
[
48
, Proposition 4.3]
with
q=2
and
(S_{0},S_{1})
replaced by
(T_{n},\tau_{n,j})
, we have for
|x|\leq C_{X}
,

(2.63)

\displaystyle\|\Xi_{T_{n}}-Y^{n}_{\tau_{n,j}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2},C_{X}}\left\||T_{n}-\tau_{n,j}|^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{8}(\Omega)}+\left\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\Xi_{T_{n}}]\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau_{n,j},T_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\||T-S_{j}|^{\tau}\right\|_{L^{8}(\Omega)}+\left\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\Xi_{T_{n}}]\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau_{n,j},T_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

To estimate the second term on the right-hand side,
note that Assumption
(T)
yields

\left\|\Xi_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\|T-T_{n}\|^{\frac{l}{2}}_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

and

\left\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\Xi_{T}]\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau_{n,j},T_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\left\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\Xi_{T}]\|_{p\text{-var};[S_{j},T]}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\|T-S_{j}\|^{\frac{l}{2}}_{L^{1}(\Omega)},

and thus by the same argument leading to (
2.51
), we get

(2.64)

\displaystyle\left\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\Xi_{T_{n}}]\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau_{n,j},T_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\left\|\Xi_{T_{n}}-\Xi_{T}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\|\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[\Xi_{T}]\|_{p\text{-var};[\tau_{n,j},T_{n}]}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\|T-T_{n}\|^{\frac{l}{2}}_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|T-S_{j}\|^{\frac{l}{2}}_{L^{1}(\Omega)}.

Using Assumption
(T)
again, we get

(2.65)

\|\Xi_{\tau_{n,j}}-\Xi_{T_{n}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim_{\Theta_{2}}\|T-T_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{l}{2}}+\|T-S_{j}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{l}{2}}.

Thus, by (
2.63
), (
2.64
) and, (
2.65
